\ui{Images} and \ui{Galleries} filter direct assignments over the same visual
tag vocabulary. A shared tag can appear in both; its edit still affects every
assignment. Counts and usage sorting follow the selected scope. \ui{All tags}
includes unassigned tags. \ui{Documents} is a disabled placeholder for future
PDF tagging; direct requests explain that document editing is not yet available.

\ui{Admin > Edit tags} maintains the shared tag vocabulary. Selecting a tag
exposes its canonical lowercase name, stable identifier and description. For
visual tags the identifier forms the public tag URL and the description can
appear on that page; music tags use internal metadata. Usage references link
to the related public gallery, photo editor or song editor. Counts in the
catalog follow the selected usage filter rather than always combining scopes.

Renaming a~tag or~changing its slug updates the~shared tag record, so~every gallery/photo assignment continues to~reference that one reusable tag. Names and~slugs are~normalized to~the~safe tag convention on~save and~a~duplicate slug is~rejected. Deleting a~tag is~different: the~application transactionally detaches it~from both galleries and~photographs and~then removes the~tag record. Use \ui{View public tag} to~review the~landing page and~\ui{Configure tag display} to~open the~Theme controls for~tag-page/card and~hero-tag presentation.

\subsection{Feature switches}
\index{feature switches}
\index{Features!Admin}

The~specialized destinations for~public live search, lightbox browsing,
Smart Galleries, gallery PDF documents and~gallery music use compact,
translucent controls. Central Settings shares this treatment across categories;
Smart Gallery editors retain it~inside the~side panel. Preference switches keep
their native keyboard operation, with larger tap targets on~narrow screens.
Settings question marks open help beside the~control; Escape or~an~outside
click closes it. Each specialist retains its existing Save workflow; catalog
row selections remain ordinary selection boxes.

Dashboard \ui{Maintenance > Content and~display} uses a~denser layout:
one toolbar and~compact groups flow into one to~three columns according
to~available width, retaining each group's own height. Groups match Features:
quiet outlines, translucent surfaces, tinted headings with a~left accent and~thin
row separators. Question marks retain
the~explanations; counts and~warnings stay visible. Switches save immediately
without confirmation or~a~Save button; their tooltips state the~factory default.
Saving, success and~failure remain visible. Unverified completion requires
a~state reload instead of~replaying the~write. Without JavaScript, native help
disclosures and~the~existing Save/POST forms remain available.

This is~the~default pattern for~new or~revised Admin preference interfaces,
specified in~\path{docs/ADMIN_DESIGN.md}. Use switches with immediate saving,
compact headed groups and~one to~three independently sized columns. The~reference
uses a~preferred column width of~300 CSS pixels, 38-pixel heading strips
and~26~by~14-pixel switch tracks; narrow/touch interaction targets are~at~least
44 pixels high. A~setting with its own configuration page has~a~clickable name
and~a~trailing settings arrow leading to~the~same destination. Name, switch
and~question mark remain independent navigation, preference and~help controls.
Apply the~pattern as~each owning interface is~edited; older forms may~still await
migration.

\ui{Appearance > Features} presents optional application features in~compact
grouped tables. Whole groups flow into one, two or~three columns according
to~the~available workspace width, reading down each column before the~next.
Groups keep their own heights and~are~never split between columns; narrow
pages use one column. Switches sit directly before feature names and~save
immediately, without a~second confirmation or~Save action.
The~tinted group header switches
every editable feature in~that group; a~mixed switch indicates that only some
are~configured on. Linked feature names open their specialized settings where
available. The~plain \ui{?} opens contextual description, configured/effective
state, dependencies and~technical details; Escape or~an~outside click closes it.

Each row shows its factory value and~whether the~configured preference differs.
Factory means the~explicitly declared new-installation default, otherwise
the~registry default; it~can~differ from an~upgrade compatibility default.
\ui{Reset to~factory defaults} above the~table immediately saves all editable
switches to~those factory values. Feature data and~specialized preferences
are~preserved. A configured switch can~remain on~while a~disabled dependency
makes the~feature unavailable; enabling that dependency restores availability.

Saving, saved and~failure feedback stays visible. A group write can~partially
succeed if~a~domain owner refuses a~later setting; the~table then displays
the~authoritative persisted state. If~a~response is~lost, \ui{Reload state}
reads the~server before permitting another change and~never repeats
the~uncertain write. Without JavaScript, the~complete form retains an~explicit
Save action and~factory reset uses a~normal authenticated POST.

A~disabled feature is~hidden from~normal navigation and~its guarded routes refuse the~feature workflow. Disabling a~switch does~not~delete the~feature's code, photographs, database records, credentials, or~configuration. Re-enabling it therefore restores the~normal entry points subject to~the~same schema, access, and~configuration checks as~before. Examples of~switchable features include public search, lightbox browsing modes, Picture Manager, Inline Administration on~public pages, Smart Galleries, voting, Picture Game, downloads, gallery and~flight maps, navigation data, SimBrief, OpenAI assistance, local AI metadata, API uploads, mobile WebDAV, gallery migration, Media Renamer, and~anonymous telemetry.

\subsection{Gallery management}

Gallery administration covers the~full life of~a~collection: creation, discovery, naming, description, dates, tags, privacy, passwords, branding, covers, layout, ordering, moving, and~eventual deletion. A~child gallery can~inherit useful choices from its parent. For~example, every gallery inside a~private family branch can~follow the~parent's protection, and~an~individual child can~use its own presentation choice where the~editor offers an~override. The~bulk gallery screen provides corresponding multi-gallery operations where the~same change is~appropriate for~several collections at~once.

Gallery descriptions can~present safe external links written as~ordinary Markdown links, \codeword{[link=URL]label[/link]}, \codeword{[url=URL]label[/url]}, or~the~short forms whose visible text is~also the~target. Only HTTP and~HTTPS targets are~linked; other schemes remain inert text. Recognized services use bundled local brand symbols. For~other public hosts, saving the~gallery can~retrieve a~small validated favicon into the~local gallery cache. Retrieval is~hostname-deduplicated, bounded by~size, time, redirect, and~retry limits, rejects private or~reserved network destinations, and~never occurs while a~visitor renders the~public description. If~the~cache or~its database table is~unavailable, the~link remains usable without an~icon.

Link targets retain underscores and~query parameters. Emphasis in~labels and~literal inline code remain intact, and~links stay in~the~surrounding paragraph.

When clean public-path storage is~available, \ui{Regenerate clean public paths} rebuilds the~stored gallery and~image paths from~the~current hierarchy and~reports separate gallery/image counts. Reordering and~selected editor saves also refresh paths when their changes require it. This maintenance action is~useful after hierarchy repair, migration, or~older imports; it~does not~turn URL rewriting on~by~itself, so~query-string routing remains the~fallback when rewriting is~disabled or~unsupported by~the~host.

\subsection{Metadata organizer}
\index{metadata organizer}
\index{EXIF!date organizer}
\index{gallery!automatic organization}

The~gallery editor includes an~\ui{Organizer} workflow for~turning a~flat set of~direct photographs into date-based child galleries. The~current implementation groups by~EXIF capture date already stored in~the~image database. Location-based secondary grouping is~reserved for~future use and~is~not~an~active organizer strategy in~Version~\version{}.

Choose minimum and~maximum EXIF dates, then build a~preview. Previewing reads database metadata in~small batches and~does not~scan, rename, or~move source files. The~draft shows the~number of~direct photographs, matching candidates, proposed child galleries, and~photographs ignored because they have~no~usable EXIF date or~fall outside the~chosen range. A~minimum date is~particularly useful for~excluding photographs from~a~camera whose clock was~unset.

For~each accepted calendar date, the~proposed child folder name is~the~stable ISO date \codeword{YYYY-MM-DD}. Its~display title uses the~gallery date-display formatter and~falls back to~a~human-readable day/month/year value if~that formatter cannot supply a~label. Before proposing a~new child, the~organizer looks for~an~existing direct child with the~same organizer title and~reuses it. This makes a~repeated preview/apply cycle append to~the~same daily destination instead of~creating another equivalent day gallery.

Applying the~draft requires a~separate confirmation. PHP Gallery creates or~reuses the~proposed date child galleries and~moves matching photographs through the~same physical move service used by~the~ordinary bulk image mover. Originals and~known generated derivatives therefore move together with the~database ownership update. Large applies are~split into bounded browser-driven requests instead of~one long PHP request. Review the~draft and~maintain a~backup before reorganizing a~large gallery.

\subsection{Media renamer}
\index{Media Renamer}
\index{filenames!renaming}
\index{media maintenance}

Choose the~scope, set the~filename pattern, then build the~preview before confirming any physical rename. The~compact gallery list supports local search without changing checked selections; it~is~shown for~the~checked-gallery scope. For~a~single gallery, use the~dropdown. Question-mark controls reveal detailed help. The~preview shows summary counts and~expandable gallery groups; open each group to~review its~old-to-new mappings and~statuses before applying. Search terms are~combined with AND regardless of~their order; enclose a~phrase in~double quotes to~match its~word order exactly. Visibility filters refresh the~gallery selector without generating a~rename plan. Build the~plan explicitly with Preview rename plan. Empty galleries occupy compact summary rows; galleries with file mappings expand within a~bounded result list. A~visible progress bar accompanies requests: list refresh and~preview show indeterminate activity, while batched checks and~renames report measured progress.

\ui{Galleries > Media renamer}, and~the~renamer panel available for~an~individual gallery, can~normalize physical image filenames from~gallery context and~the~current photo order. This is~a~filesystem operation, not~a~cosmetic title change, so~the~tool always begins with a~dry-run plan. The~preview lists each old and~proposed filename and~marks files that already match, are~missing, would~collide, or~must be~skipped by~a~safety rule.

Only direct image files inside the~selected gallery folder are~eligible. Missing sources, paths outside the~gallery, and~unsafe targets are~not~renamed. If~the~preferred target name is~already occupied but~a~safe deterministic suffix can~resolve the~collision, the~preview shows that adjustment. Applying a~plan requires confirming that the~preview was~reviewed.

The~default pattern is~\codeword{\{gallery_path\}_\{seq4\}}. A~custom pattern may~use \codeword{\{gallery_path\}}, \codeword{\{gallery_title\}}, \codeword{\{photo_title\}}, \codeword{\{old_name\}}, \codeword{\{old_stem\}}, \codeword{\{image_id\}}, and~sequence placeholders \codeword{\{seq\}} through \codeword{\{seq5\}}. The~numbered sequence variants pad to~two, three, four, or~five digits. Textual components are~normalized to~lowercase filesystem-safe ASCII-style underscore stems, the~source extension is~preserved in~sanitized form, and~an~empty custom pattern falls back to~the~default.

After a~successful physical rename, PHP Gallery keeps the~database row and~public path references aligned, updates derived titles where the~renamer owns them, moves or~invalidates generated derivatives as~appropriate, and~removes stale cached ZIP archive records that refer to~the~old source identity. Derivative cleanup warnings do~not~justify reverting an~otherwise completed source rename because reproducible derivatives can~be~generated again. For~site-wide selections, the~browser advances the~work in~bounded portions and~shows the~result for~each gallery.

Temporary staging uses a~short internal filename independent of~the~original
name, so~valid long source names remain usable during a~rename. Final filenames
keep the~readable naming pattern. Sequence placeholders count the~complete
gallery order, including photographs outside the~current batch. Changing
the~gallery title preserves those positions; later uploads continue their
numbering after the~existing photographs.

\subsection{Image management}
\label{sec:media}
\index{images}
\index{uploads}

Photographs can~be~uploaded in~the~browser, copied into folders and~discovered, received through configured automation, transferred from another compatible installation, or~added through a~scoped mobile workflow. After a~photograph arrives, the~gallery records its size and~available camera information and~prepares the~smaller display copies used throughout the~site.

The~individual photo editor currently covers title, description, maintained-language variants, visibility, sort order, tags, NSFW / 18+ state, the~private editorial rating used by~Smart Gallery rules, optional per-photo responsive-thumbnail bounds, and~read-only EXIF/GPS details. Where local AI metadata exists, the~editor shows the~generated model/source, searchable internal text, and~raw metadata JSON separately from~the~public caption. OpenAI controls insert reviewable drafts into editable text fields and~do~not~replace saved editorial text automatically.

Bulk image operations are intentionally narrower than the~individual editor and~perform server-side ownership checks again. Current bulk actions include:

\begin{itemize}
  \item move selected photographs to~an~existing gallery;
  \item create a~new destination gallery and~move the~selection into it, with cleanup of~an~empty newly created destination if~the~move fails before files are~committed;
  \item delete selected photograph records together with their owned originals and~known generated derivatives through the~normal deletion service;
  \item set a~selected photograph as~the~gallery title/cover image;
  \item change photo visibility among the~supported draft/public/private states;
  \item turn per-photo NSFW protection on~or~off when its schema is~known;
  \item create thumbnails for~the~selected photographs.
\end{itemize}

Photo ordering can~be~changed by~dragging the~row handle, and~the~gallery can~be~sorted by~filename or~EXIF capture date. Photos without a~capture date stay last, and~changes are~saved through the~normal ordering route.

When opening \ui{Edit gallery} in~the~right Admin drawer, ordinary settings remain usable while photos load in~the~background. A~thin progress strip counts processed thumbnails; \ui{Images} stays unavailable until the~complete list and~all thumbnail requests have settled. Failed thumbnails receive placeholders and~an~explicit \ui{Retry images} action; settings can~still be~saved. Only the~small table thumbnails are~preloaded. Large previews remain on~demand, and~the~File renamer prepares its~plan when its~tab is~opened. Closing or~replacing the~drawer cancels obsolete work. Ordinary full-page navigation remains the~complete fallback for~browsers without JavaScript.

The~gallery editor's \ui{Images} tab keeps common photo work in~one compact list. Accessible thumbnail previews can~be~opened by~pointer, keyboard focus, or~click; row selection supports a~Shift-click range. Each row provides direct controls for~visibility, choosing the~gallery title picture, editing, and~deleting that photo. These actions continue to~use the~existing authenticated server workflows. The~\ui{Show file names} option changes only the~editor display; administrators can~save an~optional browser-wide default, while each gallery remembers its~choice for~the~current editor session.

The~title-picture star has three states when the~database supports multi-photo covers: an~outline star selects that photo alone (gold); clicking the~gold star again keeps it selected and~arms accumulation (blue); clicking another outline star adds it to~the~selection, up~to~four photos. Clicking any selected blue star keeps only that photo (gold). Each click saves immediately. The~right drawer updates the~stars in~place, preserving its~open tab, scroll position, other form drafts, and~loaded image rows; the~public gallery preview refreshes separately. After a~lost or~failed response, the~editor first reads the~authoritative cover state without changing the~panel. If the~state changed, it reconciles the~stars before refreshing the~public gallery; otherwise read the~current state before retrying, because persistence may have succeeded before response construction failed.

Under \ui{Media management} > \ui{Title picture}, \ui{Automatic (2 photos)} chooses up~to~two suitable direct photos and~fills missing positions from accessible child galleries. The~original/default mode preserves legacy behavior for~galleries that have not explicitly selected Automatic or~a~manual cover. Public cards recheck current visibility and~gallery access; unpublished or~inaccessible photos are~not~revealed.

When the~optional \ui{Picture Manager} feature is~enabled, a~normal signed-in administrator can~select photographs and~direct physical subgalleries in~the~public gallery grid. Checkmarks select individual cards; Shift-click selects a~range; Ctrl-click or~Cmd-click toggles a~card; \ui{Select all}, \ui{Clear}, and~Ctrl/Cmd+A support larger visible-page selections. Smart Gallery cards are~not~part of~this file-based selection. Dragging an~unselected photo starts with that photo as~the~selection, while dragging an~existing photo selection moves the~selected photographs together. A~visible child-gallery card becomes a~drop target, and~dropping there performs the~same validated photo move as~the~destination picker. Physical gallery cards themselves are~not~drag sources.

From the~toolbar the~administrator can~delete a~mixed selection or~create a~real physical gallery from it. Creation accepts a~searchable parent-gallery choice, copies selected photographs into the~new root, and~copies selected physical subgallery trees below it while leaving the~sources unchanged. The~server writes current gallery sidecars, rejects recursive destinations and~existing target folders, reserves each target root before copying, rebuilds copied gallery rows from~the~filesystem, and~removes the~new partial result if~the~operation fails. Mixed deletion removes selected photographs through normal image cleanup and~applies the~configured recoverable Trash policy to~selected gallery trees when available.

Move and~copy-to-existing-gallery remain photo-only actions. If~a~physical gallery is~selected, clear it~before using those controls. Photo selections can~also be~shared or~downloaded. \ui{Share} fetches the~authorized browser-displayable image versions as~browser \codeword{File} objects and~opens the~native Web Share sheet when the~browser/device supports multi-file sharing. The~browser chooses the~available receiving apps, so~PHP Gallery cannot force Instagram Story, Reel, or~another specific target. If~native sharing is~unavailable, cannot accept the~files, or~fails after preparation, the~manager starts the~authenticated selected-photo ZIP download as~a~fallback. The~same ZIP endpoint can~therefore serve both an~explicit selected-photo download and~share fallback. Ordinary visitors never receive these controls, and~the~server revalidates the~logged-in account, CSRF token, source ownership, direct-child gallery ownership, and~destination gallery for~every mutation or~ZIP request.

\subsection{Uploads and~discovery}
\index{discovery}

Filesystem discovery is~useful when photographs have already been copied to~the~server through FTP, a~hosting file manager, or~a~restored backup. The~discovery screen shows what the~application found and~lets the~administrator bring those folders and~files into the~catalog. Discovery runs as~a~session-backed browser job in~small AJAX batches rather than scanning the~entire tree during the~initial Admin page request. The~current default examines up~to~80 directories per~batch, accepts a~bounded batch size up~to~300, and~expires abandoned discovery job state after two hours. This keeps a~large folder tree responsive on~slower hosting while still showing progress and~a~final actionable result list.

The~scanner walks below the~configured gallery root and~does not~follow directory symlinks. Hidden directories and~known generated/cache directory names such as~\filepath{cache}, \filepath{thumbs}, \filepath{thumbnail}, \filepath{thumbnails}, \filepath{preview}, and~\filepath{previews} are~ignored. A~new candidate branch must contain at~least one supported image somewhere below it. Empty structural parent folders can~still become galleries when they are~needed to~preserve the~hierarchy above image-bearing descendants. A~folder containing only a~\filepath{gallery.json} sidecar and~no~supported image anywhere in~its branch is~reported as~metadata-only rather than imported as~a~gallery by~the~current discovery workflow. Already indexed gallery folders are~suppressed from the~candidate list, and~an~import candidate whose display title would duplicate an~existing sibling title is~not~silently created.

For~each unmanaged result, the~administrator can~import the~folder tree in~place, move supported photographs from selected unmanaged folders into an~existing gallery, or~delete selected unmanaged folders from~disk. Import expands the~selection in~parent-to-child order and~can~include missing image-bearing ancestors and~descendants. The~move action avoids overwriting an~existing destination filename by~choosing a~deterministic unique name, scans the~destination gallery after successful moves, and~removes source directories only when they become empty. The~discovery-specific disk-delete action is~deliberately restricted to~safe non-root unmanaged paths below the~gallery root and~refuses folders that are~already represented by~database galleries. Use the~normal gallery deletion workflow for~an~imported CMS gallery.

\subsubsection{The~gallery.json metadata sidecar}
\index{gallery.json}
\index{sidecar metadata}

PHP Gallery keeps its active catalog and~relationships in~the~database, while an~optional \filepath{gallery.json} file beside a~gallery folder preserves useful editable metadata with the~filesystem. Normal gallery saves write the~sidecar back to~the~gallery folder. Current sidecar output includes the~title, description, normalized gallery tags, visibility, order, voting and~filename-display state, maintained content language/translations when available, manual gallery dates, selected card/count/lightbox/grid overrides, responsive-thumbnail bounds, access/listing policy, cover references, and~configured gallery branding assets where those schemas are~available. Sidecar writing follows the~same schema checks as~the~corresponding gallery features.

During discovery and~missing-parent repair, PHP Gallery can~read supported values from~the~sidecar instead of~deriving everything from the~folder name. Imported rows conservatively default to~unpublished when no~visibility is~specified. The~current import path restores the~sidecar fields explicitly supported by~the~importer, including title/description/localization, visibility, manual dates, voting/filename presentation, selected card/count/lightbox/grid/thumbnail overrides, access/listing policy, order, and~branding paths. The~sidecar should therefore be~treated as~portable gallery-folder metadata, not~as~a~complete database backup: relational state, credentials, logs, votes, game state, Smart Gallery definitions, and~other database-owned records still require the~normal backup procedure.

\securitynote{The~\ui{Delete from disk} action on~the~discovery screen is~a~real recursive filesystem deletion for~unmanaged candidate folders. Review the~selected relative paths before confirming it. The~guard against known imported gallery paths is~an~additional safety boundary, not~a~replacement for~a~backup.}

Browser upload is~the~friendliest choice for~routine work. It~can~upload into an~existing gallery or~create a~child gallery and~upload optional photographs in~the~same side-panel workflow. Select the~destination, choose files, and~follow the~progress display. The~application checks the~destination and~file information before accepting each result.

With JavaScript enabled, focus the~upload area and~paste an~image or~screenshot using Ctrl+V on~Windows/Linux or~Cmd+V on~macOS. The~focused or~most recently used visible uploader receives the~images; an~open side panel owns its upload target. Paste appends all supported image items exposed by~the~browser to~the~same file selection used by~the~chooser. JPEG, PNG, GIF and~WebP follow the~picker policy; HEIC, HEIF and~DNG additionally require an~explicit server-capability hint. Browser-provided filenames are~retained; unnamed image data receives a~distinct timestamp/sequence name with the~matching extension. Submit the~ordinary form to~upload through the~selected browser/server pipeline, with its usual validation, size limits, metadata, thumbnails, progress and~errors. Text fields keep ordinary text paste; unsupported content and~paste during an~active upload are~ignored. If~the~browser cannot expose or~add clipboard images, use the~chooser or~file drop. Side-panel uploads retain the~open panel and~current URL.

In~the~right-hand upload drawer, repeated paste, file-picker choices and~drop append to~one local preview queue. Inspect its count and~total size; use Remove, Clear selection or~the~arrow buttons to~adjust the~FileList and~upload order. Then press the~ordinary Upload button once. Both existing-gallery and~create-with-upload drawers use this queue; the~dedicated full-page picker keeps its established behavior. The~queue does not~create a~server draft and~has~no~Commit publication action.

Preview work is~lazy and~serialized: one decoder, at~most twelve miniature resources, and~PNG miniatures no~larger than 256 pixels per~edge. Before decoding, only the~first 256 KiB of~headers are~inspected; a~source must fit within 24 MiB, 8192 pixels per~edge and~16 * 1024 * 1024 pixels overall. Unsupported, animated, unreadable or~larger candidates show an~icon or~warning while the~original remains selected. These preview limits do~not~replace server upload limits. Selection never strips EXIF or~replaces the~original with a~miniature.

Repeated filenames receive a~warning but~remain separate selected occurrences; removing a~duplicate updates that warning. Empty files, unavailable previews and~format/size warnings do~not~erase healthy files. A~disabled file input also blocks clipboard and~drop additions. The~displayed total describes the~local selection: classic uploads send one image per~request and~browser preparation follows its configured batch limits.

Closing or~switching the~drawer offers an~explicit choice for~unsent files; Escape cancels that warning and~restores focus. Native Reset clears only an~unsubmitted selection. After an~uncertain or~partially failed upload, removal, replacement and~Reset are~blocked to~preserve the~exact retry inputs and~operation keys. Retry from the~same form; only canonical acknowledgment clears the~queue. Navigation releases preview resources. A~browser history-cache return may~restore page state, but~an~ordinary reload does not~retain unsent files.

Compressed file size alone does~not~prove that an~image is~safe to~decode. Before GD or~optional Imagick allocates a~full raster, PHP Gallery checks the~format, dimensions, pixel arithmetic, configured/runtime memory budget, and~pipeline limits. A~file that cannot~be~admitted safely is~refused before its original or~generated derivatives are~committed to~the~target gallery. This policy applies to~classic and~browser-prepared ingestion; it~does not~claim that every external RAW decoder has~the~same in-process memory model.

The~upload settings distinguish the~ordinary server path from~browser-assisted preparation. \ui{Allow all server-supported formats} leaves the~picker aligned with~the~server's detected capabilities. \ui{Prefer phone-rendered JPG/PNG/WebP, no RAW/DNG} asks mobile browsers for~a~rendered image representation when possible, which is~useful when an~iPhone ProRAW/DNG workflow produces unsuitable color. The~browser hint is~not~a~guaranteed transcoder, so~the~server still validates the~actual uploaded media.

Browser-assisted preparation is~enabled independently and~is~checked by~default in~the~upload workflow. The~browser can~prepare optimized thumbnails and~bounded ZIP batches before sending them. If~browser preparation fails before any server-side write starts, ordinary image selections can~fall back automatically to~the~classic server upload path. Administrators can~bound the~default and~maximum browser worker counts, images per~batch, ZIP-to-PHP-upload-limit ratio, maximum ZIP batch bytes, and~source chunk size used by~browser-side thumbnail rebuild. The~normalizer also enforces a~hard worker cap so~an~accidental setting cannot create unbounded parallel requests on~shared hosting.

Server-side acceptance of~browser-prepared ZIP batches also verifies the~CRC-32 of~every stored file and~requires a~complete central directory and~end record matching entry names, offsets, sizes, and~counts. A~damaged or~inconsistent prepared batch is~rejected before gallery files are~written; rebuild and~retry the~batch in~the~browser.

Current browser-pipeline defaults are~8 workers, an~80\% target of~the~detected PHP upload limit for~each store-only ZIP batch, at~most 8 images per~ZIP batch, and~a~24~MiB absolute ZIP-batch cap. The~validated ranges are~1--32 workers, 10--95\% of~the~PHP upload limit, 1--64 images per~batch, and~1--128~MiB for~the~absolute ZIP cap. Browser thumbnail rebuilding uses a~512~MiB source-ZIP chunk by~default, clamped to~16~MiB--3~GiB, and~the~source builder normally caps a~chunk at~96 items with an~internal hard limit of~512. These are safety ceilings and~defaults, not~a~recommendation to~raise a~slow shared host to~the~maximum.

When browser-assisted upload is~enabled, the~chooser also accepts ZIP archives such as~an~iCloud Photos export. The~browser inspects the~archive locally, adds supported JPEG, PNG, WebP, and~GIF images to~the~ordinary preparation queue, and~skips folders, hidden metadata, and~unsupported entries. The~selected ZIP itself is~never uploaded to~PHP. Stored and~Deflate archives are~supported; encrypted, multi-disk, ZIP64, damaged, suspiciously compressed, or~excessively large archives are~rejected before any server-side upload begins. ZIP import has~no~classic-PHP fallback, so~keep browser-assisted preparation enabled when selecting an~archive.

The~global automatic-upload rename option can~run the~same post-scan naming policy used by~the~Media Renamer after browser, API, or~WebDAV ingestion. Leave it~off when external filenames are~part of~your archival convention; enable it~when all arrival paths should converge on~the~gallery's normalized naming scheme.

\subsection{Generated metadata and~automation}
\index{AI metadata}
\index{OpenAI}
\index{upload automation}
\index{WebDAV}

The~optional local or~worker-based image-analysis queue can~record visual metadata separately from the~human title, description, tags, and~EXIF fields. Administrators can~review that metadata in~the~photo editor and~use it~as~searchable evidence without silently replacing editorial text. Configured OpenAI assistance can~generate a~photo or~gallery description, clean up~existing text, summarize context, describe visible content from safe generated thumbnails, or~suggest a~translation. These actions insert reviewable drafts; they do~not~publish or~save text automatically unless the~administrator completes the~ordinary save operation.

For~repeated transfers, create a~scoped upload-automation token for~the~intended gallery and~send supported files to~the~upload endpoint. Tokens can~be~revoked independently and~must be~treated like passwords. The~raw API key is~shown only when it~is~created; only its hash is~stored afterward, so~copy the~new key to~the~intended client at~creation time. The~global \ui{API manager} lists automation credentials across galleries and~provides a~central revocation workflow without exposing the~original secrets. Gallery-level controls remain available for~the~same scoped credentials.

The~Windows companion uses the~same gallery-scoped upload contract. It~has~two normal upload modes. \ui{Watch folder} ignores files that were already present when watching begins, waits for~new files to~become stable, remembers confirmed uploads locally, and~retries transient failures with backoff. It~can~optionally attach the~current Microsoft Flight Simulator camera or~aircraft latitude/longitude/altitude through SimConnect immediately before each watched upload, and~can~delete a~watched source only after the~gallery confirms a~successful upload. \ui{Manual upload} accepts a~file selection independently of~the~watch folder and~can~either generate responsive thumbnails on~the~PC with worker processes or~ask the~gallery server to~generate them. Thumbnail work and~network uploads are~pipelined rather than~staging an~entire large selection first. Tray support lets the~watcher or~manual job continue while the~main window is~hidden.

The~same Windows application can~optionally run a~low-priority AI metadata worker. It~claims one authenticated analysis job, downloads only the~claimed asset, produces internal searchable metadata with the~selected local backend/model generation, and~returns the~result to~the~existing upload-automation endpoint. This worker is~separate from~human captions. A~gallery-level \ui{Force AI metadata regeneration} action clears the~stored generated rows/old queue state for~that gallery branch and~queues fresh work; the~heavy analysis still runs on~the~Windows worker.

Mobile WebDAV credentials are~gallery-scoped and~use HTTP Basic authentication plus an~unguessable path token. Creating a~connection shows its generated password once; the~server keeps only a~password hash afterward. The~Admin page lists the~connection label, target gallery, server URL, and~last-use time and~can~revoke the~connection. The~WebDAV endpoint supports the~minimal \codeword{OPTIONS}, \codeword{PROPFIND}, \codeword{MKCOL}, and~\codeword{PUT} behavior needed by~photo-transfer clients such as~PhotoSync. Current WebDAV ingestion accepts JPG, PNG, GIF, and~WebP. Configure the~mobile client to~convert HEIC to~JPEG before transfer where available. Uploaded media then enters the~same scan, optional auto-rename, database, and~thumbnail pipeline as~other uploads.

\section{Thumbnail generation and~public rendering}
\label{sec:thumbnails}
\index{thumbnails}
\index{WebP}
\index{JPEG}
\index{image previews}
\index{page speed!thumbnails}

\subsection{What a~thumbnail is}
\index{thumbnail!definition}

A~thumbnail is~a~smaller display copy of~a~photograph. The~original photograph may~be~several thousand pixels wide and~occupy many megabytes. A~gallery card on~a~phone may~need only a~few hundred pixels. Sending the~full original for~every small card would make visitors wait for~detail they cannot see at~that size.\footnote{Thumbnail generation keeps the~source photograph as~the~archival and~full-viewing asset. The~generated copy is~a~replaceable browsing aid.}

Imagine a~gallery containing 60 photographs, each with a 12-megabyte original file. Loading every original for~the~first grid could require hundreds of~megabytes. Small thumbnails can~reduce that first viewing task dramatically. The~original remains available for~authorized full viewing or~download, while the~grid receives efficient previews.\footnote{Actual savings depend on~image content, quality, format, dimensions, and~the~browser's selected candidate. The~example illustrates scale rather than promising one fixed compression ratio.}

Thumbnails serve several everyday purposes:

\begin{itemize}
  \item gallery grids appear sooner;
  \item scrolling uses less memory and~network capacity;
  \item mobile visitors receive an~image closer to~the~size of~their screen;
  \item gallery covers and~search results remain compact;
  \item the~large viewer can~begin with a~suitable preview while a~larger source is~prepared;
  \item repeated visits can~reuse files already stored in~the~browser cache.\footnote{A~browser cache stores recently used web resources on~the~visitor's device according to~server and~browser policy. Reuse can~make a~later visit feel much quicker.}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Why several thumbnail sizes exist}
\index{thumbnail!sizes}
\index{device pixel ratio}

One small size cannot suit every screen. A~narrow phone card may~look clear with a 300-pixel thumbnail. A~wide desktop card may~need 600 or 800 pixels. A~high-density screen uses more image pixels to~draw the~same physical area and~may~benefit from a~larger candidate.

PHP Gallery can~prepare common sizes such as 300, 600, 800, 960, 1280, and 1600 pixels, depending on~configuration and~source-image limits. The~number describes the~maximum long side of~the~generated copy. Portrait and~landscape photographs keep their original proportions.\footnote{For~a~landscape photograph, the~long side is~usually the~width. For~a~portrait photograph, it~is~usually the~height. The~shorter side is~calculated from the~original aspect ratio.}

The~smaller candidates serve grids and~covers. Larger candidates serve wide cards, high-density displays, search presentations, map previews, and~the~large viewer. The~browser can~select from the~available group instead of~receiving one fixed size for~every situation.

\subsection{Why JPEG and~WebP are~available}
\index{thumbnail!formats}
\index{JPEG!thumbnail use}
\index{WebP!thumbnail use}

JPEG is~widely compatible and~familiar for~photographs. WebP often provides similar visual quality with fewer transferred bytes. PHP Gallery can~generate both so~a~compatible browser can~use WebP while JPEG remains a~dependable alternative.\footnote{Compression efficiency varies with the~photograph and~quality settings. Fine texture, noise, gradients, and~sharp graphic details can~produce different results.}

The~visitor does not~choose a~format manually. The~page tells the~browser which versions exist, and~the~browser chooses a~format it~understands. The~original photograph keeps its own source format and~remains separate from these browsing copies.

\subsection{When thumbnails are~created}
\index{thumbnail!generation}
\index{thumbnail!maintenance}

Thumbnails can~be~prepared when photographs are~imported or~uploaded, rebuilt through Admin maintenance, or~generated through a~guarded repair process when the~public page discovers that an~expected copy is~missing. Preparing them near upload time gives later visitors the~smoothest experience because the~required files already exist before the~first visit.\footnote{Large imports can~require meaningful processing time and~storage. Running preparation as~an~intentional Admin task gives the~owner a~clearer opportunity to~monitor completion.}

A~gallery owner should review thumbnail maintenance after:

\begin{itemize}
  \item importing a~large existing folder tree;
  \item changing enabled thumbnail sizes or~quality;
  \item restoring a~backup that contains originals and~database data but~an~incomplete thumbnail cache;
  \item moving an~installation between servers with different image-format support;
  \item seeing repeated original-file fallbacks or~missing previews in~System Health.
\end{itemize}

Generated thumbnails are~reproducible cache data and~can~be~rebuilt from the~source photographs. The~sources themselves require permanent backup protection.

\subsection{DNG and~RAW display masters}
\index{DNG}
\index{RAW photographs}

DNG photographs can~remain the~archival source while PHP Gallery creates a~browser-display master for~thumbnail generation and~public viewing. The~display master is~generated derivative data, not~a~replacement for~the~original DNG. If~the~required local conversion tool is~unavailable or~conversion fails, the~Admin upload and~thumbnail reports identify the~failed DNG derivative. Thumbnail maintenance can~rebuild the~master later; deleting generated derivatives does~not~delete the~source DNG.

\ui{Runtime diagnostics} exposes the~active DNG conversion policy together with the~server's actual GD, Imagick/ImageMagick, and~format capabilities. The~source policy can~use automatic fallback with full RAW decoding first and~the~embedded camera preview second, explicitly prefer full RAW decode, or~prefer the~embedded preview. Color handling can~force browser-safe sRGB, preserve the~decoded appearance as~closely as~possible, or~use camera white balance when available. These choices affect generated browser derivatives only. They never rewrite the~archival DNG source, and~their availability still depends on~the~image delegates and~memory limits provided by~the~host.

\subsection{What visitors receive}
\index{thumbnail!browser selection}

The~gallery sends the~browser a~list of~thumbnail copies that really exist for~a~card. The~browser considers the~space available on~the~page, the~sharpness needs of~the~screen, the~supported format, and~its own loading decisions. It~then requests an~appropriate file.

For~example, suppose a~photograph has 300, 600, 800, and 960-pixel copies:

\begin{itemize}
  \item a~small phone card may~receive the 300-pixel copy;
  \item a~medium card may~receive the 600-pixel copy;
  \item a~large or~high-density card may~receive the 800 or 960-pixel copy;
  \item the~large viewer may~request a~still larger preview prepared for~that purpose.
\end{itemize}

The~exact choice can~differ between browsers and~screens. This flexibility is~useful because the~server does not~need to~guess one universal size.

\subsection{Responsive browser selection}
\index{responsive renderer}
\index{srcset}
\index{thumbnail!responsive mode}

\ui{Responsive browser selection} is~the~supported legacy mode under \ui{Admin > Theme > Layout}. The~page exposes the~available thumbnail candidates immediately and~the~browser chooses the~one that best matches the~rendered card and~display density. JavaScript is~not~required for~this selection.\footnote{The~underlying web standard calls the~candidate list a~\emph{srcset}. Gallery owners can~rely on~the~visual behavior without learning that markup term.}

Responsive selection usually transfers one chosen thumbnail for~each loaded card and~is~the~normal choice when predictable browser-native behavior matters most.

\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.30\textwidth} X}
\toprule
\textbf{Situation} & \textbf{Likely result}\\
\midrule
Narrow mobile card & A~smaller thumbnail candidate is~usually sufficient.\\
Wide desktop card & The~browser may~choose a~medium or~larger candidate.\\
High-density display & A~higher-resolution candidate may~be~selected for~the~same CSS size.\\
JavaScript unavailable & Responsive candidate selection still works.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}

The 300-pixel copy acts as~a~fallback when available. Its presence does not~force a~modern browser to~download it~first because the~larger choices are~presented at~the~same time.

\subsection{Progressive thumbnail sharpening}
\index{progressive renderer}
\index{progressive sharpening}
\index{thumbnail!progressive mode}

\ui{Progressive thumbnail sharpening} is~the~default and~safe fallback under \ui{Admin > Theme > Layout}. The~gallery first presents a~small thumbnail, usually 300 pixels, and~later replaces relevant cards with larger copies. On~slower connections this can~make the~collection recognizable earlier, at~the~cost of~potentially transferring both the~small and~replacement thumbnail.

\subsubsection{What happens on~the~screen}

\begin{enumerate}
  \item The~page reserves the~final card geometry.
  \item Small thumbnails begin filling visible cards.
  \item Relevant cards are~queued for~a~larger candidate.
  \item The~larger copy loads while the~small one remains visible.
  \item The~card is~replaced after the~larger copy is~ready.
\end{enumerate}

Larger upgrades are~queued rather than started for~every card at~once, and~visible cards receive priority over distant content. The~current progressive option applies to~photo cards inside an~open gallery. Gallery covers, collage cells, home-page gallery cards, and~Admin images continue using responsive browser selection. The~Admin interface labels progressive sharpening as~Default and~responsive selection as~Legacy.

\subsubsection{The~transfer tradeoff}

Progressive mode can~transfer two files for~one photograph: the~small first copy and~the~sharper replacement. Responsive mode often transfers one browser-selected copy. Progressive mode therefore favors earlier visual population, while responsive mode usually reduces total transfer for~the~same card.

\subsection{Which mode should an~owner choose?}
\index{thumbnail!choosing a renderer}

Choose \ui{Responsive browser selection} when efficient browser-native selection and~typically one thumbnail transfer per~loaded card suit the~collection.

Keep \ui{Progressive thumbnail sharpening} for~a~small first image followed by~bounded upgrades on~relevant cards. Test both supported renderers with representative galleries and~devices when choosing the~best presentation for~your visitors.

Useful questions include:

\begin{itemize}
  \item Do~visitors see the~first photographs sooner?
  \item Does the~small version look acceptable during sharpening?
  \item How much extra data is~transferred on~a~typical visit?
  \item Do~phones and~high-density screens sharpen at~a~comfortable pace?
  \item Does the~chosen mode suit the~audience's likely connection quality?
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Loading priorities and~stable layout}
\index{lazy loading}
\index{layout stability}
\index{thumbnail!lazy loading}

The~first visible photographs receive earlier loading attention because they shape the~visitor's first impression. Photographs farther down the~page use lazy loading, which means the~browser can~wait until they approach the~visible area. This avoids spending connection capacity on~the~bottom of~a~long gallery while the~visitor is~still looking at~the~top.\footnote{Lazy loading timing belongs partly to~the~browser. Browsers consider scrolling, viewport distance, connection state, and~their own implementation policy.}

The~gallery knows the~width and~height of~indexed photographs. It~uses those proportions to~reserve stable card shapes before image loading finishes. Text, buttons, and~neighboring cards therefore keep their positions as~thumbnails appear.

Visitors who prefer reduced motion receive a~calm presentation without a~required continuous loading animation. Photograph links remain usable throughout loading and~sharpening.

\subsection{Thumbnail quality and~storage}
\index{thumbnail!quality}
\index{thumbnail!storage}

Higher quality preserves more fine detail and~can~create larger files. Lower quality saves space and~transfer while introducing more visible compression. The~best setting depends on~subject matter, screen use, hosting capacity, and~audience. Detailed foliage, hair, text, and~fine architecture can~reveal compression more readily than broad soft backgrounds.

Every additional enabled size and~format uses storage because it~creates another copy for~each photograph. The~benefit is~more precise delivery to~different screens. System Health and~storage statistics help an~owner understand the~resulting cache. A~large source archive may~produce a~substantial thumbnail collection, yet~those files remain reproducible from the~originals.

\subsection{Thumbnail compatibility and~maintenance modes}
\index{thumbnail compatibility}
\index{thumbnail maintenance}
\index{browser-side thumbnail rebuild}

The~maintenance panel has~two compatibility policies. \ui{Modern} generates WebP thumbnails wherever the~server can~write WebP. \ui{Legacy} generates JPEG fallback thumbnails as~well as~WebP. After switching an~installation permanently to~Modern, \ui{Remove legacy JPG thumbnails} deletes only generated JPEG thumbnail files; originals, WebP derivatives, and~DNG display masters are~preserved.

A~full \ui{Check missing thumbnails} pass inspects imported photographs and~records galleries with missing, stale, metadata-missing, or~wrong-ratio generated variants without creating anything. The~stored result enables \ui{Create missing thumbnails}, which targets only the~reported images. \ui{Create all thumbnails} in~Maintenance covers all galleries; the~same action in~a~gallery's Images editor covers that gallery and, when \ui{Include subgalleries} is~selected, its descendants. These Admin operations remain the~primary deliberate maintenance workflow. Public browsing does~not~start an~unbounded repair sweep, but~the~current renderer has~a~small guarded background warmup for~specific cards that had~to~fall back to~authorized original media because an~expected derivative was~missing.

\ui{Refresh thumbnail database} reconciles database metadata with existing generated files and~removes wrong-ratio generated files instead of~publishing them. \ui{Delete all thumbnails} is~a~separately confirmed destructive cache operation; source photographs remain untouched and~the~derivatives can~be~rebuilt later.

\ui{Browser-side thumbnail rebuild} is~selected by~default for~these regeneration actions when browser processing is~available. Clear its checkbox to~use server generation. The~server sends original files to~the~authenticated browser in~bounded source ZIP chunks, the~browser generates thumbnail variants, and~prepared thumbnail ZIP batches are~uploaded back for~server validation and~installation. The~source-chunk and~batch limits are~configured with the~browser-upload settings so~the~workflow does~not~depend on~one oversized PHP request.

\subsubsection{Guarded public thumbnail warmup}
\index{thumbnail warmup}
\index{thumbnail!self-healing}

When the~server has~rendered a~publicly authorized photograph through an~original-media fallback, it~can~mark that exact photograph and~the~missing supported sizes as~a~background warmup candidate. The~browser waits for~idle time, stops when the~page is~hidden, sends at~most two candidates per~request, makes at~most four requests from one page load, and~spaces requests by~about 1.8 seconds. The~server independently caps the~normalized submitted list at~24 image IDs, but~the~actual generation budget is~smaller: an~anonymous request processes at~most two source images within roughly four seconds, while an~authenticated administrator request processes at~most four within roughly eight seconds.

A~candidate carries an~HMAC token tied to~the~image ID, gallery ID, filename, relative path, and~gallery folder that the~server originally rendered. Before generating anything, the~endpoint reloads the~image/gallery, verifies that token, repeats current visitor access checks, normalizes the~requested sizes to~the~configured thumbnail set, consults thumbnail metadata when available, and~skips variants that are~already renderable. A~per-image 60-second cooldown prevents repeated expensive retries. A~single global non-waiting worker lock means concurrent public traffic cannot stack several image-decoding jobs; a~busy request simply reports that another warmup worker is~active. The~warmup is~enabled by~default through the~internal \setting{thumbnail_background_warmup_enabled} application setting and~writes bounded diagnostic events to~the~Admin log when logging storage is~available.

\securitynote{Background warmup does not~turn a~private or~otherwise inaccessible photograph into~a~public repair target. The~signed candidate proves that the~server rendered that image as~a~fallback candidate, and~the~endpoint still repeats the~current access policy before opening the~source or~writing derivatives.}

\subsection{If~a~thumbnail is~missing}
\index{thumbnail!missing}
\index{thumbnail!rebuild}

A~missing thumbnail may~appear as~a~slower original-file fallback, an~empty preview, or~a~maintenance warning, depending on~the~image and~available files. Open the~thumbnail maintenance tools, inspect the~affected gallery or~sizes, and~rebuild the~missing variants. Review write permissions and~image-format support when rebuilding repeatedly fails.

After repair, open the~gallery with an~empty browser cache and~confirm that covers, cards, and~the~large viewer receive the~expected previews.

\section{Lightbox, maps, EXIF, and~voting}
\label{sec:lightbox}
\index{lightbox}
\index{EXIF}
\index{GPS}
\index{voting}

\subsection{Compact browsing and~keyboard focus}

The~photo viewer fits narrow screens, with the~counter separated from~its
controls. Opening a~photo or~map moves keyboard focus into~that layer;
\ui{Tab} and~\ui{Shift+Tab} stay inside it. \ui{Escape} closes the~map before
the~underlying photo, and~closing returns focus to~the~opening control.
At~100\% a~single finger swipes between photos; above that scale one finger
pans and~two fingers pinch. Closing a~direct photo restores the~album context
for~language switching and~login return links. Map actions without available
location data are~hidden. Interactive markers identify route starts,
destinations, waypoints or~photo locations.

Tag results use readable single-column cards on~narrow screens. Search
suggestions show plain description text while retaining useful link labels.
Authored photo captions/titles take precedence over a~localized numbered
fallback. Public controls and~media/map panels follow the~selected Theme's
foreground and~panel colors. The~picture game retains an~album breadcrumb
link and~compact photo comparisons. Floating navigation uses small icons;
widgets retain their saved placement and~return to~the~page flow when
previews or~controls leave no~safe floating space.

\subsection{Asynchronous lightbox metadata}

The~lightbox is~the~large photograph viewer that opens above the~gallery page. It~lets visitors concentrate on~one photograph while retaining forward, backward, close, full-screen, slideshow, map, and~other available controls.

The~gallery prepares the~viewer when it~becomes useful and~obtains information for~nearby photographs in~manageable groups. A~visitor can~therefore move through a~large gallery without requiring the~first page to~carry every title, preview, map point, and~vote at~once.\footnote{The~current viewer normally requests information in~nearby groups of~photographs. Access checks are~repeated for~each request so~private gallery rules continue to~apply.}

The~Left and~Right arrow keys move one photograph backward or~forward. \codeword{Shift+Left} and~\codeword{Shift+Right} move ten positions for~faster travel through a~large result set. The~jump follows the~same stable ordered sequence as~ordinary navigation, wraps at~the~ends, and~also works when the~active result set is~a~Smart Gallery. The~translated keyboard-help text lists these shortcuts; no~separate graphical jump buttons are~added.

The~viewer usually starts with an~appropriately sized preview. A~larger or~original source can~be~used where the~action and~access policy call for~it. Nearby previews may~be~prepared quietly so~the~next photograph appears smoothly.

While a~new photograph is~being prepared, the~viewer uses the~same progress area that reports full-quality loading. Rapid forward or~backward movement always gives the~newest requested photograph ownership of~that indicator, so~an~older preview or~metadata request cannot leave the~viewer permanently busy or~replace the~current selection. If~a~photograph cannot be~presented, the~progress area shows a~recoverable failure message; the~visitor can~continue with the~forward, backward, close, or~reopen controls. These loading states do~not~change gallery access, protected-media, Smart Gallery, map, slideshow, or~no-JavaScript rules.

\subsection{Adjusting slideshow speed}

Start the~slideshow with \codeword{S} or~its existing control. Move the~pointer to~reveal two compact outlined rows: \ui{Photo change speed} and~\ui{Transition speed}. Each range offers \ui{Fast}, \ui{Medium}, and~\ui{Slow}; the~current choice appears beside it. Both ranges run from Slow on~the~left to~Fast on~the~right. Arrow keys adjust a~focused range without navigating photographs. Actual input restarts the~inactivity timer. All controls, including optional developer diagnostics, disappear after inactivity even when a~range retains focus or~the~pointer rests over it. Dragging keeps controls visible until release; movement reveals them again.

On~mobile, both speed ranges and~the~soundtrack panel hide with the~idle
controls. Tap the~photograph to~reveal them again; photos and~music keep playing.

Photo display intervals are~1, 2, and~4 seconds; transition durations are~0.5, 1, and~2 seconds. Medium preserves the~existing defaults. Changes apply immediately and~independently: the~interval replaces the~pending wait, while the~next blend uses the~new transition duration. An~animation already running finishes normally, and~the~next photograph still waits for~its full-quality image to~be~ready. Choices remain during stop/resume and~close/reopen within the~same viewer instance; reloading restores Medium. No~Save action is~needed.

\subsection{Zooming and~panning a~photograph}
\index{lightbox!zoom}
\index{zoom controls}
\index{pinch gesture}

The~lightbox zoom range is~100--400\%. Use the~minus and~plus controls, or~select the~percentage control to~return to~100\%. Keyboard shortcuts are~\codeword{+} or~\codeword{=} for~zoom in, \codeword{-} or~\codeword{\_} for~zoom out, and~\codeword{0} for~reset. Focus indicators and~the~current percentage remain exposed to~assistive technology.

On~desktop, normal mouse-wheel movement and~two-finger trackpad scrolling pan an~enlarged photograph in~both axes; at~100\% they do~not~change the~fitted image. A~trackpad pinch or~\codeword{Ctrl} plus wheel zooms the~photograph around the~pointer, while \codeword{Command} plus wheel remains a~browser shortcut. On~touchscreens, a~two-finger pinch keeps its midpoint fixed and~one finger pans an~enlarged image. At~100\% a~one-finger horizontal swipe navigates photographs; switching from swipe to~pinch and~from pinch to~pan does~not~advance the~gallery.

At~100\%, the~photograph is~fitted and~centered in~the~viewer. Full-screen mode uses the~same zoom and~pan behavior, and~its Close and~other controls remain clickable while the~image is~enlarged.

Zooming above 100\% immediately requests the~protected full-quality source for~the~active photograph. The~existing preview remains visible until the~larger image is~ready, and~the~current zoom and~pan position are~preserved. In~normal, full-screen, and~mobile full-screen modes, this transfer shows the~same progress bar with a~percentage and~a~byte count. Changing photographs, closing the~viewer, or~starting a~newer full-quality request cancels the~previous transfer. A~failed or~cancelled full-quality request leaves the~protected preview in~place.

Navigation, slideshow start, close, and~reopen return the~viewer to~a~centered 100\%. Entering or~leaving full screen keeps the~current scale when the~photograph itself has~not~changed and~adjusts pan to~the~new viewport. Reduced-motion preferences remove the~short discrete zoom transition.

Zoom affects presentation only. It~does not~alter the~stored photograph or~bypass media authorization. Password, share-link, NSFW, Smart Gallery, map, voting, pagination, and~lightbox metadata rules continue to~apply.

\subsection{EXIF and~map policy}

EXIF is~information many cameras and~phones save inside a~photograph. It~can~include capture date, camera, lens, exposure, dimensions, orientation, and~GPS coordinates. PHP Gallery can~use this information for~date suggestions, maps, technical details, ordering assistance, and~duplicate review.

Maps load when a~visitor asks to~see them, which saves work for~visitors interested only in~the~photographs. A~gallery can~show individual photo locations and~an~overview of~available points. Parent and~child settings determine which galleries expose this information. The~global EXIF/GPS public-display setting is~the~fallback for~galleries whose override is~set to~inherit. Admin also shows how many galleries currently have~an~explicit override and~can~reset all of~those overrides back to~inherit in~one confirmed settings save. The~reset is~schema-guarded: if~the~override state cannot~be~verified, the~application refuses the~change and~directs the~administrator to~System Health instead of~guessing.

Selecting \ui{Open photo} in~a~gallery-map marker opens that photograph in~the~current lightbox when it belongs to~the~active gallery. This also works when the~photograph is~outside the~currently displayed pagination page: the~viewer requests only a~bounded metadata window around the~selected photograph. In~full-screen split-map mode the~map stays open while the~photograph pane changes. From a~normal map overlay, the~map closes and~reveals the~selected photograph in~the~viewer. If~the~enhanced viewer cannot resolve the~selection, the~same control follows the~canonical photograph page; it does not~link directly to~the~raw image file.

\securitynote{GPS data can~reveal sensitive locations. Administrators should review inherited map settings, image metadata, and~public access before publishing personal photographs.}

A~gallery with a~saved flight route can~open its map before the~first photograph is~uploaded, including pages with no~lightbox markup. The~map fits all stored route points and~retains ordinary reset controls without treating route points as~photo markers. Without stored coordinates it~shows the~normal empty-map message. Gallery access and~effective map settings still apply; loading remains on~demand.

Leaving photo fullscreen with \codeword{F}, its~control, or~the~browser's
\codeword{Escape} closes the~split map and~returns to~the~ordinary lightbox
with the~same photograph and~zoom. Pending map loading cannot reopen the~pane
after exit. In~CSS fullscreen, one \codeword{Escape} performs the~same
transition. \codeword{M} toggles the~map independently while fullscreen;
a~later \codeword{Escape} closes the~ordinary lightbox.

\subsection{Flight maps and~navigation data}
\index{SimBrief}
\index{flight maps}
\index{navigation data}

Admin > Maintenance > Maps and~navdata uses the~compact translucent group
treatment from~Features and~Content and~display. The~navdata name and~trailing
arrow open the~same manager. Counts and~the~last import time remain visible;
question-mark help and~Import details hold longer explanations. Update navdata
remains an~explicit action with in-place progress and~the~normal form fallback.

Flight-oriented collections can~use the~optional SimBrief integration to~retrieve the~latest operational flight plan for~a~Pilot ID or~pilot name. The~compact editor accepts one visible identifier: digits alone select the~Pilot ID path; other text selects the~pilot-name path. Existing integrations that submit separate fields remain compatible, with Pilot ID preferred when both are~provided. The~editor creates an~editable Markdown description and~route draft; the~OFP and~available route coordinates attach after the~gallery is~saved. A~failed SimBrief request leaves the~ordinary description field and~manual route editor available. The~pre-create draft is~private and~temporary as~described in~the~gallery creation section.

The~SimBrief preview fills the~existing route editor with the~complete filed route, adding missing departure and~arrival airports once while preserving route instructions and~airport/runway tokens. Description drafts in~all maintained languages keep the~complete route, including its destination on~long routes. The~status explains that the~OFP and~available map points will attach when you~save the~gallery; importing alone does~not~save them. If~you~change the~route, identifier, description, or~source language while the~request runs, the~response preserves your form values and~asks you~to~import again. After saving, the~map retains the~original OFP coordinates.

A~gallery with a~saved local SimBrief OFP PDF offers \ui{View flight plan (OFP)} and~\ui{Download OFP PDF} in~its description, even with no photographs. The~PDF opens in~its own locally rendered document lightbox; its page controls never navigate through gallery photographs. \ui{Fit whole page} is~the~initial mode, sizing each page to~both the~usable stage width and~height and~centering it clear of~the~header and~toolbar. Choose \ui{Fit width} for~width-first display with vertical scrolling, or~\ui{Actual size (100\%)} for~the~PDF screen-pixel scale. The~active mode is~indicated on~the~toolbar. Zoom in/out, 100\% zoom reset to~the~last chosen fit, touch pinch/pan, keyboard page navigation, and~fullscreen remain available.

\paragraph{Historical OFP attachments and pre-dispatch review.}
\index{SimBrief!retrospective OFP}
For~an existing gallery without a~valid OFP PDF, open the~administrator's \ui{API} editor tab and~use \ui{Create historical OFP / Open prefilled SimBrief}. A~modal first shows the~selected gallery and~editable departure/destination ICAOs, date, UTC time, aircraft type, flight details, route, flight level, alternate and~passengers. Verified saved OFP values are~labelled as~such; route-map fields and~a~single gallery date are~marked uncertain, while unknown values stay blank. The~overlay does~not~infer an~original flight from gallery titles or~EXIF timestamps. \ui{Cancel / Back} causes no~navigation, provider request or~gallery change.

The~review dialog also works above the~open right-hand Admin panel without closing that editor. Its~fields remain editable, \codeword{Tab} moves between them, and~\codeword{Escape} or~\ui{Cancel / Back} closes only the~review dialog and~returns focus to~the~editor.

Even with~a~valid PDF already attached, \ui{Prepare revised OFP in SimBrief} opens this~same editable review without changing the~stored document. After generating and~downloading the~new PDF on~SimBrief, use the~separate confirmed \ui{Replace existing OFP PDF} upload. The~\ui{Origin of the uploaded PDF} selector only records provenance; choosing a~retrospective origin never creates or~downloads a~file. The~departure-date picker has its~own full-width row so~the~native control remains visible in~the~modal on~narrow screens.

Enter the~cruise flight level as~hundreds of~feet, for~example \codeword{350}; the~redirect sends the~documented \codeword{fl=FL350} representation. An~unsigned browser reaches the~SimBrief login screen before the~dispatch fields. Reaching that screen does~not~verify acceptance of~a~historical date; check the~date after signing in~and~before generating.

Only \ui{Open SimBrief Dispatch} sends the~reviewed, URL-encoded options to~SimBrief's official HTTPS dispatch page in~a~separate protected browser tab. The~administrator may need to~sign in and~must choose \ui{Generate} on~that site. No~developer key, OAuth or~background request is~needed. Historical dates use the~documented DDMMMYY format, but SimBrief may reject or~adjust old dates. A~newly generated retrospective plan is~not the~original flight-day OFP: weather, NOTAMs, AIRAC, routes and~fuel can~differ.

After downloading the~new PDF, return to~the~same gallery's \ui{API} tab and~use \ui{Upload OFP PDF} to~attach one validated document, at~most 25\,MiB. Select whether it~is~a~retrospective generation or~a~manually supplied document. A~valid existing PDF instead offers \ui{View OFP}, \ui{Download PDF}, and~a~separate collapsed \ui{Replace existing OFP PDF} workflow requiring explicit confirmation. The~canonical OFP manifest records provenance; gallery dates, descriptions, media, routes, visibility, and~original OFP JSON stay unchanged. This is~a~single-gallery action, not~a~bulk rebuild.

The~saved PDF is~served through the~gallery-scoped \codeword{gallery\_ofp\_pdf} route on~the~same browser origin as~the~gallery. Its~\codeword{index.php} query links also work in~subdirectory installations without URL rewriting. A~fresh anonymous visitor can~view and~download an~OFP of~a~public or~unpublished gallery via its~direct URL without administrator login. Unpublished means unlisted, not private: the~original PDF has the~same visibility as~the~gallery page, including password, share-token, and~NSFW access gates. Truly private source galleries without a~valid visitor grant and~generated private OFP-page child galleries remain protected. A~verified administrator may access a~private source PDF except in~anonymous preview mode. Forbidden, missing, and~invalid PDFs return the~same uncached plain-text \codeword{404}, without rendering an~HTML gallery page or~revealing filesystem paths. Older \codeword{media} URLs with \codeword{ofp=1} remain compatible.

The~compact \ui{View flight plan} and~\ui{Download OFP PDF} buttons share one row. An~administrator can~optionally create a~private OFP-page subgallery from a~separate action below them. A~localized question-mark help button explains the~conversion on~hover, keyboard focus, or~tap without occupying space in~the~gallery description.

Automatic whole-page and~width fits recompute for~each PDF page's actual dimensions and~orientation, window resizing, device rotation, and~fullscreen transitions. Manual zoom retains its chosen CSS scale and~pan during ordinary resizes; selecting a~fit mode or~resetting zoom restores automatic fitting. The~automatic calculation excludes the~previous manual pan margins, so~reset and~width/page fitting keep the~PDF readable and~proportional even after resizing a~zoomed document. When document rendering fails, the~viewer offers the~original protected PDF. The~existing gallery visibility, password, and~NSFW authorization rules still guard viewing and~downloading. In~CI, the~required Chromium job retains the~detailed \codeword{browser-map.log} as~an artifact whenever the~document-viewer browser contract needs diagnosis.

Left and~Right arrows move by~one PDF page; holding either arrow continues at~a~controlled rate after the~previous page finishes rendering. Navigation stops at~the~first or~last page without wrapping. Releasing the~key, leaving the~viewer or~changing fullscreen ends the~held-key sequence. Page navigation preserves the~chosen fit or~manual zoom. Editable controls retain their arrow keys; \codeword{Tab} and~\codeword{Shift+Tab} move through the~viewer controls with visible focus, without outlining the~whole PDF stage.

The~\codeword{F} shortcut and~the~fullscreen button control only the~OFP document viewer; \codeword{Escape} first leaves fullscreen and~then, in~normal view, closes the~document. Typing in~editable fields, modifier shortcuts, and~repeated keys do~not~activate fullscreen. If~the~browser refuses a~native fullscreen request, the~same viewer uses a~CSS-only fullscreen layout. In~fullscreen, the~header and~toolbar float above the~PDF, fade after a~short period of~pointer inactivity, and~reappear on~pointer or~keyboard activity. Hovered or~keyboard-focused controls remain visible; showing the~controls does~not~resize or~rescale the~PDF.

Normal mouse-wheel movement and~two-finger trackpad scrolling pan only the~PDF page, including horizontal scrolling and~\ui{Fit width}; they do~not~zoom the~document, viewer controls, or~browser. \codeword{Ctrl} plus wheel or~a~trackpad pinch zooms the~PDF at~the~pointer, while the~browser retains \codeword{Command} plus wheel. On~phones and~tablets, one finger pans the~document and~two fingers pinch to~zoom; the~remaining finger can~continue panning after a~pinch. The~PDF HUD keeps a~fixed dark, high-contrast palette independent of~the~gallery theme and~stays at~its original scale in~both normal and~fullscreen modes. \ui{Fit whole page}, \ui{Fit width}, and~\ui{Actual size (100\%)} restore automatic display.

Manual route text is~resolved offline-first. Imported OurAirports database rows are~checked first, then the~small bundled CSV fallback. A~valid cached provider result may~be~used next; when an~administrator has~linked a~configured Navigraph account and~remote lookup is~allowed, an~unknown identifier can~be~resolved through Navigraph and~cached under the~known AIRAC cycle. The~Navigation Data manager shows local database and~bundled counts, provides a~lookup tester for~identifiers such as~airports/navaids/fixes, and~can~refresh the~local OurAirports airport/navaid dataset. Navigraph OAuth/account storage is~optional and~can~be~disconnected without removing local data.

With JavaScript enabled, entering or~focusing a~nonempty route, requesting a~SimBrief draft, or~saving route-bearing input also checks local navigation-data freshness in~the~background. Both \ui{Flight Maps} and~\ui{Navigation Data} must be~enabled. OurAirports data is~refreshed when the~last successful import is~at~least a~week old, with an~hour between failed attempts. Gallery saving does~not~wait for~this request; the~editor draft, open side panel, and~browser address stay in~place. A~refresh failure still allows the~gallery to~be~saved.

After the~check, unresolved saved manual routes can~gain missing coordinates from~current navigation data. Existing coordinates and~SimBrief OFP geometry are~preserved, and~a~newer route edit or~deletion prevents the~background result from~overwriting that state. Only saved routes are~completed; unsaved browser text is~not~sent with the~refresh. Changed public map contexts are~refreshed through the~shared panel workflow. Import and~route writes require verified database storage; missing or~uncertain storage prevents the~optional write while ordinary gallery editing remains available.

Public map rendering never calls SimBrief, Navigraph, or~another live planning service. It~renders only coordinates already stored with the~gallery. Saved SimBrief OFP coordinates therefore remain usable even if~the~remote provider is~later unavailable. Manual route text can~also bypass identifier lookup completely with explicit \codeword{NAME@latitude,longitude} points.

\subsection{Voting}

Public cards and~lightbox controls share image-vote state. The~current public vote is~a~reversible positive vote: pressing the~active up-vote again removes it. The~displayed score is~the~sum of~stored image votes; there is~no~separate whole-gallery voting endpoint in~the~current implementation. Logged-in votes are~associated with the~account, while anonymous votes use the~application's privacy-preserving visitor hash. New positive votes are~rate-limited; revoking an~existing vote is~allowed immediately. Server-rendered forms provide direct behavior, while JavaScript submits normal interactions asynchronously and~synchronizes the~score across matching views. The~server validates image identity, gallery policy, access, schema readiness, CSRF, and~request integrity.

\subsection{Picture Game}
\index{Picture Game}
\index{image comparison}

Picture Game is~an~optional public comparison mode. An~administrator enables it~for~a~gallery branch; eligible public photographs from~that gallery and~its descendants can~then participate subject to~the~ordinary access and~visibility rules. The~public game shows two photographs side by~side, records which pair was~presented, and~lets the~visitor choose the~preferred image before moving to~the~next comparison. The~gallery can~also present leading results accumulated by~the~game.

Because presenting a~new pair creates persistent game state even before the~visitor chooses a~winner, Picture Game requires its database schema to~be~known and~available. If~the~required storage cannot~be~verified, the~write is~refused rather than~silently falling back to~an~untracked comparison. The~global feature switch can~hide Picture Game without deleting existing game data or~per-gallery enable settings.

\section{Duplicate Photo Detector}
\label{sec:duplicates}
\index{Duplicate Photo Detector}
\index{duplicate ledger}
\index{SHA-256}

The~Admin Duplicate Photo Detector analyzes a~selected gallery branch by~default and~can~expand to~global scope when the~administrator enables the~corresponding option. It~reports exact and~possible duplicate pairs, provides gallery and~photo context links, records review decisions, and~delegates confirmed deletion to~the~normal gallery image-deletion service.\footnote{The~detector remains a~review tool. Evidence supports administrator judgment because matching file size and~photographic metadata can~describe distinct photographs under some workflows.}

\subsection{Evidence categories}

\subsubsection{Exact and~possible findings}

An~exact duplicate contains the~same file content. The~application recognizes this through a~checksum, which acts like a~very detailed digital fingerprint calculated from the~file. Matching fingerprints provide strong evidence that two files contain the~same bytes.

A~possible duplicate has~supporting similarities without an~exact fingerprint match. The~detector can~compare file size and~available camera information such as~dimensions, capture time, camera, lens, exposure, aperture, focal length, ISO, and~orientation. Two separately exported copies of~one photograph may~differ as~files while retaining enough shared information to~deserve review.

Missing camera information simply gives the~detector fewer clues. The~results remain suggestions for~a~person to~inspect. Open both photograph links, compare their gallery context and~quality, and~delete only the~copy whose removal fits the~collection.

\subsection{Persistent review ledger}

The~ledger belongs to~the~authenticated administrator. Pair entries store canonical image identifiers, with the~lower identifier in~the~low column and~the~higher identifier in~the~high column. Gallery entries apply to~the~exact stored gallery. Parent and~child gallery decisions remain independent.

Available review actions include:

\begin{itemize}
  \item ignore the~selected pair;
  \item ignore all findings from the~left image's exact gallery;
  \item ignore all findings from the~right image's exact gallery;
  \item clear the~current administrator's ledger;
  \item delete a~confirmed duplicate through validated normal image deletion.
\end{itemize}

AJAX actions refresh the~detector fragment in~the~existing Admin right-side panel. Direct POST requests use redirect behavior as~a~fallback. Server-owned scan jobs preserve scope between continuation requests, and~deletion prunes the~affected image from current job results.

\section{Access control and~security}
\label{sec:security}
\index{security}
\index{CSRF}
\index{authentication}
\index{NSFW}

\subsection{Layered access evaluation}

Privacy is~evaluated before protected output is~assembled.
